I know most people will look at that gesture and say, “Oh, you are so rich. You must have so much money that you are willing to give ten million dollars just to make her smile.” But a few will reach the heart of the gesture and say, “You must love her so much. You must love her so much that one smile from her is worth ten million dollars to you. She must mean the world to you.”

That is the difference. The reality is that I love her so much that I am willing to pay ten million dollars to the person who makes her smile.

So when Allah says that He will give the reward of ten years of اعتکاف to the one who fulfills Asif's need, the common reading of that HADEES becomes a motivation to act for that reward. Yet most people miss what it reveals: how much Allah loves me. Ya Allah, You love me so much that You would give that much reward to anyone who fulfills my need and removes my constriction for me. SubhanAllah.

So this HADEES can also be heard another way: “O one who feels low. O one who feels depressed. O one who feels burdened and constricted by troubles. It is you I am looking at. It is you for whom I am opening doors, because I want to see you happy. For your happiness, I will reward anyone with this amount of ثواب if they strive to bring you ease.”

This is the رحمۃ He has for us. AllahuAkbar.

My perspective changes completely when I see another person in need. I begin to see that this is a person Allah loves. He loves him so much that He is willing to reward me with immense generosity and immense blessings for helping him. Then I ask myself what place I may gain in His sight if I bring ease to someone He loves.

When someone does something deeply kind for a person I love, that person becomes dearer to me. He comes closer to my heart. In the same way, once I understand this view, it is nearness to Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى that moves me to help another person, more than the reward itself.

I have to appreciate why such immense rewards are attached to such simple acts. They come from the love He has for us. Because of that love, He showers His blessings upon even the smallest acts of kindness. He may grant the reward of ten or twenty Hajj for a few hours of worship. I may act for the reward, or I may act out of gratitude for that divine love.

### Responding in afflictions

I will give another example. There is a HADEES in which Rasul Allah ﷺ tells us that when I visit a sick person, I should ask him to pray for me and for my forgiveness.

At first, that sounds reversed. I would expect myself to be the one praying for the sick person, asking Allah to grant him health. He appears to be the one in need of my prayers. Yet the HADEES says that I should ask him to pray for me and for my forgiveness.

What does that mean? I can make it clearer with an example.

My daughter may fall very ill, God forbid. May Allah protect our children from trials. I give this only as an example to make the point clearer. She becomes very sick and is admitted to the emergency room. The doctors come to me and say that she is in severe pain and cannot breathe properly.

As a father, I cannot even imagine the state I would be in if that ever happened. Nothing is more painful for a parent than seeing his own child suffer, whether the suffering is physical, mental, or spiritual. Then the doctor tells me that she cannot breathe and must be put on a ventilator.

At that moment, there would be no other place in the world where I would want to be. I would not leave my daughter's side even for a second. I would sit beside her because I love her so much. I would remain at her bedside, constantly praying for her.

Then Allah answers my prayers. She wakes up. She says, “Babu! I want ice cream.”

I would be ready at once. “You want ice cream? Of course. What flavor do you want?” If I could not find it there, I would drive wherever I had to drive to get that ice cream for her. That is what I would do.

At that point, I would feel intensely close to her. “What else do you want, sweetheart? Is there anything else I can do for you? Is there anything else I can get for you?”

It so happened that, just before she fell ill, we had a terrible fight. She had disobeyed me and spoken to me very rudely. I was still angry with her when all of this happened.

Then she wakes up and remembers what she did. She says, “Babu, I'm sorry for what I did and said. Please forgive me!”

I know what I would say: “Forgive you? Sweetheart, there's nothing to forgive. I'm not upset with you at all anymore. Don't even think about it.”

That is how parents are with their children. There is nothing strange in it.

If this is how deeply I love my child, then Allah's love for his creation is beyond what I can imagine. According to a HADEES, Allah holds the love of seventy mothers for every single one of his creation. In such an affliction, I felt intensely close to my daughter. When a creation of Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى is going through trials and afflictions, Allah is nearer to him than I can grasp.

I was ready to respond to anything my daughter asked for in that moment, though my ability is extremely limited. But when a human being, a creation of Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى, is in pain and asks for something, whether for himself or for someone else, Allah does not refuse him in that moment.

When I am afflicted, Allah is closest to me in that very moment, closer than any human being could ever be. When I turn to Allah for forgiveness with true repentance, there is nothing He would not forgive, whatever sin I may have committed and however terribly I may have behaved. Allah would forgive instantly.

I do not hesitate for a second to forgive my child. The mercy of Allah is far greater. He is the One who makes Himself known through love. He holds the love of seventy mothers for His creation, so He does not refuse forgiveness.

If I were angry with my son, and my daughter woke up and said to me, “Babu! Please forgive my brother,” would I refuse? “Oh my God, you are in such a difficult state. You are in so much pain. You are suffering so much right now, and even in this state you are concerned about your brother? You want me to forgive him? Your request will surely be granted. Of course I forgive him. I will give you anything you want right now. All you have to do is ask.” SubhanAllah

I can now appreciate the beauty of that HADEES. In the very middle of my affliction, I raise my hands and ask Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى to fulfill another person's need. I may ask for my own forgiveness, or for the forgiveness of someone else. At that moment, there is no way Allah turns down my request, because my trial has brought me extremely close to Him. He is right by my bedside, holding my hand. That is how His رحمۃ manifests.

This is why trials become such a perfect opening for me to ask Allah for what I need. A trial can become one of the best experiences a person ever has: an experience of awareness, nearness, and closeness to the love of Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى.

If Allah is so loving, why does He afflict people in the first place?

> وَمَآ أَصَبَكُم مِّن مُّصِيبَةٍۢ فَبِمَا كَسَبَتْ أَيْدِيكُمْ وَيَعْفُوا۟ عَن كَثِيرٍۢ

## Linguistic Meaning Of Rabb

I begin, InshaAllah, with the linguistic meaning of the word رب. Before that meaning, there is a striking sign from the جمال: the beauty of the Quran. It belongs to the symmetrical beauty of the Quran, especially the symmetry in its use of the words Allah and RAB.

### Symmetry of Allah and RAB

> بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ ‎۱‏ٱلْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِرَبِّ ٱلْعَلَمِينَ ‎۲‏ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ ‎۳‏مَلِكِ يَوْمِ ٱلدِّينِ ‎۴‏إِيَّاكَ نَعْبُدُ وَإِيَّاكَ نَسْتَعِينُ ‎۵‏ٱهْدِنَا ٱلصِّرَطَ ٱلْمُسْتَقِيمَ ‎۶‏صِرَطَ ٱلَّذِينَ أَنْعَمْتَ عَلَيْهِمْ غَيْرِ ٱلْمَغْضُوبِ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا ٱلضَّآلِّينَ
>
> ( 1 ) In the Name of Allah, the Most Beneficent, the Most Merciful.

( 2 ) All praises and thanks belong to Allah, the Lord of the *'Alamin*. ( 3 ) The Most Beneficent, the Most Merciful. ( 4 ) The Only Owner of the Day of Recompense. ( 5 ) You we worship, and You we ask for help. ( 6 ) Guide us to the Straight Way. ( 7 ) The Way of those on whom You have bestowed Your Grace, not of those who earned Your Anger, nor of those who went astray.

In the first Surah of the Quran, سُورَۃُ الْفَاتِحَۃِ, I find that Allah is mentioned twice, and that RAB is mentioned once after those two mentions. The فاتحۃ begins with بِسْمِ اللَّہِ الرَّحْمَنِ الرَّحِیمِ. Allah is therefore mentioned in the first verse. Then comes اَلْحَمْدُ لِلَّہِ, another mention of Allah, followed by رب العلمین.

So the name Allah appears twice before the word RAB appears once. After that, in the remaining verses, neither Allah nor RAB is mentioned again. This is what stands at the beginning of the Quran.

At the end of the Quran, in سُورَۃُ النَّاسِ, the pattern appears from the other direction when I work backward.

> قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلنَّاسِ
>
> Say: "I seek refuge with the Lord of mankind,

In سُورَۃُ النَّاسِ, the word mentioned is not Allah but RAB: قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلنَّاسِ.

> قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلْفَلَقِ
>
> Say: "I seek refuge with the Lord of the daybreak,

When I go to the سورۃ before it, سُورَۃُ الْفَلَقِ, the word mentioned is not Allah but RAB: قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلْفَلَقِ. RAB appears again.

> قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ
>
> Say :"He is Allah, the unique!

When I go to the سورۃ before that, سُورَۃُ الْاِخْلَاصِ, the word mentioned is Allah, not RAB: قُلْ ھُوَ اللَّہُ اَحَدٌ. SubhanAllah.

In the beginning, سُورَۃُ الْفَاتِحَۃِ, Allah is mentioned twice, and then RAB is mentioned. From the end, the pattern reverses: RAB is mentioned twice, and then Allah is mentioned. SubhanAllah.

The last two Surahs use the word RAB, with no mention of the name Allah. In سُورَۃُ اِخْلَاصِ, only the name Allah appears, with no mention of RAB. The result is a perfect symmetry: one Surah at the beginning, and several Surahs at the end.

### Meaning of RAB

The root of the word رب is actually رَبَبٌ. In Arabic, the two با are combined. A شدۃ is then placed over the با, producing ربّ.

In most English translations, رب is rendered as “lord.” But the word “lord” does not carry the meaning clearly. For many English readers, it may only call to mind a title, or even a phrase such as “the Lord of the Rings.” That is why Arab linguists do not regard “lord” as a strong translation of رب. It fails to reveal the true meaning and power of the word.

RABB

I approach the word رب first through its language. The word رب is a composite word, like الہ, which has already been examined. I have shown that الہ is not used only for Allah. It can apply to many other realities in human life.

In the same way, رب is not used only for Allah. The word carries several meanings in the language. It is used for Allah, but it may also be used for human beings and for other created relations, such as parents, teachers, and guardians. The first point, then, is that رب is not specific to Allah. In Classical Arabic, رب can be used for Allah, for human beings, and for other things.

![](images/78/b8244e3f-11ba-4e9b-a182-4022d0ce982f.jpg)

The classical definition of رب is:

> الرَبُّ ھُوَ المَالِکُ وَ السَّیِّد وَ المُرَبِّی وَ المُرْشَدُ وَ المُنعِمُ وَ القَیِّمُ

I understand a true رب as one who gathers six meanings. I may also say that Allah, as رب, is all six at once: He is مالک, He is سید, He is مربی, He is مرشد, He is منعم, and He is قیم. The word RAB therefore does not carry one meaning only. It joins all six meanings together.

### al-MALIK - Owner

MALIK

![](images/78/b16edd18-ca65-470d-bddc-f05a0725a60e.jpg)

I begin with the first meaning: مالک, owner. Whoever owns something, or has authority of ownership over someone, is called رب. The thing owned is called مِلک. This is not the English word “milk,” the milk produced by cows. In Arabic, مِلک or ملکیۃ means property, and the owner of that property is called مالک.

I also find this meaning in ordinary life. Human beings own phones, iPads, computers, cars, and houses. In that limited sense, such ownership makes them مالک. Since “owner” is one of the meanings of رب, that ownership also makes a human being a رب in that sense.

I see this limited kind of ownership in teachers over their students, and in parents over their young children. Children may protest, “It is my life, Mom. You cannot tell me what to do. You do not own me.” Those feelings are common today, and, to be fair, many of us felt the same way while growing up. Still, parents do have a form of ownership over their children until those children become adults.

But this is not ultimate or absolute ownership. Parents give birth to their children, but they do not create them. Allah created the child, and Allah created the parent. Therefore, ultimate and absolute ownership belongs only to Allah. So, although parents and teachers may be رب in one sense of the word, Allah is the absolute owner and the absolute رب of His creation. He is مالک. And if He is مالک, then I am His property. I am His مِلکِیَّۃ.

### SAYYED - AUTHORITY

SAYYED

![](images/78/0b293ce1-5d98-44aa-a320-7be45f893cae.jpg)

The second meaning of the word Rabb can be seen in the word سید, which complements the earlier meaning of مالک. The word مالک means owner, while سید means someone who has authority. سید is the one in charge. The difference is simple: مالک is someone who has ownership, and سید is someone who has authority.

I may own something without having complete authority over it. That is possible. I may own a car, for example, but I still have to register it. I still have to get it inspected every year. I still have to drive within the speed limit. I still have to drive it on the road. I cannot simply drive on the sidewalk because I feel like it, unless I am in Pakistan. So, even though I own my car, I do not necessarily have complete authority over it.

The reverse is also true. I may have complete authority over something without having complete ownership of it. Take the computer in my office. I can do whatever I want with the software installed on it. I can install new programs. I can uninstall programs I do not like. Nobody will stop me or question me about that. But I do not own the computer. My company owns it.

So I may own something without having complete authority over it. And I may have complete authority over something without necessarily owning it.

Allah, as رب, has absolute ownership, and He has absolute authority. He has both at once. He may do whatever He wills with His property, whenever He wills. If He wills to burn it, He may burn it. If He wills to tear it apart, He may tear it apart. If He wills to drown it, toss it away, or discard it, He may do so by His will.

No one has the right to question Him about it, because He has complete ownership and complete authority over us and over everything else in the universe. Even complaint and grief do not bind Him. He is not obliged to heed them. They make no difference to Him.

When parents ground their children, the matter becomes easier to see. If I take my daughter's phone away, she may cry and complain as much as she wants, but she is not getting it back. In that setting, I am her مالک, and she and her phone are my ملکیۃ.

If our crying and grumbling do not move our parents, though their capacity as رب is limited, then they certainly do not compel the One who is the absolute and ultimate رب. We are creation. He is our Creator.

I constantly seem to forget that he is not the one on trial. We are. We have reversed the matter completely.

Today, mankind has placed Allah in the witness stand and taken the judge's seat. We question his every act without shame. People ask why, if he is so powerful, he does not remove evil from the world. They ask how, if he is so loving and merciful, he could allow pain to touch them. They say that they prayed, yet the job or promotion did not come. They insist that they are good people, that they paid their taxes honestly, that they paid their زکواۃ, and that they brush their teeth twice every day. Then they ask why he did this to them.

This is the reversal. The reality is that he is the judge. He is the مالک. He is the سید. We are his property and his subjects. He has every right to question us. We have no right at all to question him.

For this reason, I find it astonishing that Allah still allowed us to understand something of the wisdom in his design through the Quran and through his messengers. I see this as an immense act of mercy and generosity from him, because he is not obligated to do anything for us. He is not there to serve us. Yet he does so anyway, because that is what a true رب does.

### MURABBEE - The One Who Nourishes

I come to the third meaning of the word: مُرَبَّی. This is a powerful word. مُرَبَّی means one who ensures growth: someone who cares for a thing, nurtures it, and enables it to develop.

In other words, a مُرَبَّی is responsible for the growth and development of whatever has been placed under his care. Ownership and authority alone do not guarantee this care. I may own something and have control over it, yet neglect it. A neglected backyard makes the point plainly. People own cars but never service them. We possess our bodies, yet we often mistreat them. Ownership is present, and a measure of authority is present, but care is missing.

So ownership and authority over something do not necessarily mean that it will be properly looked after. A رب is the one who owns, has complete authority over what he owns, and also takes complete care of it. He ensures its proper growth. He nurtures it. This act of caring for what belongs to him and nurturing it is called تربیۃ. I find this phenomenon throughout the world.

TARBIYAH

![](images/78/c49b7488-8dd5-4b26-bd91-725c35cffb79.jpg)

The poet says:

> مِن دُرَّۃٍ بَیضَآءَ صَافِیَۃً || مِمَّا تَرَبَّبَ حَائِرَ البَحرٍ

I see the depth of the ocean nurturing the clear white pearl. The poet calls the ocean a رب because it helps develop the pearl inside its shell. A tree is also called a رب because it nurtures the fruit it bears.

So I find many forms of تربیۃ throughout the world. The concept of تربیۃ is tied to setting limits and drawing boundaries. That is what تربیۃ means.

The تربیۃ of a plant means caring for it through ordered limits:

- watering it at the right time
- making sure it receives enough sunlight
- protecting it from too much sunlight
- protecting it from insects and bugs

In other words, تربیۃ is a set of dos and do nots that continues until the plant matures into a tree and begins to bear fruit. If the plant is simply left to grow on its own, that is not تربیۃ, because no boundaries have been set.

In the same way, the تربیۃ of children means caring for their physical needs, their diet, and their clothing. It also means caring for their minds by educating them and protecting them. In essence, it is the close watching of their growth from birth until they mature into healthy, sound, decent human beings: physically, psychologically, and socially. This is تَرْبِیَۃُ الْاَوْلَادِ.

I do not understand تربیۃ as a quick or easy process. It is long, demanding, and full of labor. In fact, it is among the hardest forms of work: never-ending, thankless, and full-time.

This is why, when I recite التحیات in every prayer, I say: اَللّٰھُمَّ مَغْفَرٌلِی - Oh Allah forgive me و لوالدی and my parents. وَالرَّحْمُھُمَا کَمَا رَبَّیَانِی صَغِیرَۃً and have mercy on them just as they had mercy on me during the period of my تربیۃ when I was young.

In this statement, I find the primary ingredient of ربوبیۃ: mercy (رحمۃ). Mercy is the main component of ربوبیۃ. Without mercy, there can be no ربوبیۃ. Mercy is made of love, care, and concern. That is what ربوبیۃ contains.

The image of ربوبیۃ appears in the care some people give to plants. They spray each leaf with water, then clean it gently with a soft cloth. That kind of love, care, and gentleness shows what ربوبیۃ is.

It is not harshness. It is not beating. It is not psychological, emotional, or physical abuse. It is not forcing submission through guilt. It is love, patience, and kindness. That is the Islamic way of ربوبیۃ. Contrary to what appears in movies and in society today, the Islamic way of ربوبیۃ has always been رحمۃ. The proof is in the life of Rasul Allah ﷺ.

Our prophet ﷺ took the most vicious people, the most barbaric people, and transformed them without a single slap, taunt, sarcastic remark, or insult. He made them رَضِیَ اللَّہُ عَنْہُ, the greatest title a human being can receive: رَضِیَ اللَّہُ عَنْہُ.

I do not mention the names of these صحابۃ without saying رَضِیَ اللَّہُ عَنْہُ. Our prophet did that. How? Through love, gentleness, kindness, forgiveness, and patience; through his أخلاق. That is ربوبیۃ.

What did he ask of us in return? After giving twenty-three years of his life to our تربیۃ, in his last sermon, after حِجَّۃُ الْوَدَاعِ, he said:

> یا أُمَّتَی لا تُسَوِّدَ وَجْھِی یوم القیامۃ

My people! Do not embarrass me before Allah on the Day of Judgment! When the result falls short, exceptions do not decide the matter. If most students in a class fail, the principal questions the teacher: What is happening? Why have so many students failed in this class?

Our Prophet ﷺ, after twenty-three years of service and تربیۃ, asked only one thing in return: یا أُمَّتَی لا تُسَوِّدَ وَجْھِی یوم القیامۃ. Do not embarrass me before Allah on the Day of Judgment. May Allah make us among those who make our Prophet proud.

### MURSHAD

MURSHAD

![](images/78/f84f0843-7482-421c-8a2b-9747e6ab9960.jpg)

The fourth meaning of the word رب is مُرشَد. He is the one who sets things in the right direction, sets them on the right path, and puts them to proper use. This is مُرشَد.

The word مُرشَد comes from رُشد, which in classical Arabic means **to ripen**, the final stage of fruit. For this reason, رُشد also means rationality, reason, or maturity, because these are the final stages of the intellect.

So مُرشَد is the one who takes something in its raw form and works on it until it ripens and matures. Then He puts it to its proper use and sets it in the right direction.

A teacher may train a student in computer science and then also find that student a job, so the training can be applied. In that case, the teacher has acted as a مُرشَد. A مُرشَدis not someone who brings a person to maturity and then leaves that person unused. He sets the person on the path. He guides that person forward from the point of maturity. He provides the opportunity to practice what has been learned, so that the trained person and others can benefit from that learning. That is مُرشَد.

I learn from this that Allah, as رب, does not only own us, hold complete authority over us, and nurture us with love and care. He also creates opportunities for us to put our training to use. He trains us by placing people in our path who help us grow and develop. He finds the perfect friend for us, someone who helps us grow and mature. Then He guides us to put that training to use by becoming good friends ourselves to other people. That is مُرشَد.

Even parents and teachers cannot always do this. They may complete the تربیۃ of their children, but they cannot always put them to proper use. Children have to find their own path. But Allah, as رب, goes further. He sets us on the right path after our training has been completed. SubhanAllah.

### MUNIM

MUNIM

![](images/78/80747d2d-bd3b-4e95-a510-940e9d5f1391.jpg)

I take the next meaning of رب from the word مُنعِم. The word مُنعِم is used for the one who bestows gifts, the one who gives blessings. It comes from the root نِعَم or نعمۃ, which means gifts or blessings.

Slavery is no longer part of ordinary life, but the old example still makes the meaning clear. It is one thing to own a slave. It is another thing to care for him, nurture him, train him, put him to proper use, and then, beyond all of that, bestow gifts on him as well.

The word نعمۃ points to gifts and benefits given beyond what a person deserves. A gift is not something earned. By definition, it is more than what is due. It expresses the love and mercy of the giver. If something is deserved, then it is salary, payment, or earnings. It is not a gift.

Interestingly, the Arabic word نَعَم, which means yes, also comes from the same root as blessing. The connection is beautiful. The natural human response to receiving blessings is نَعَم: yes, surely. This is what I love about this language. Every word is so perfectly connected to its root.

I may own a slave, or an animal, such as a goat. If I own a goat, I take care of it. But I do not buy it gifts. I do not buy it jewelry. There is no need. It is a goat. It is part of my property.

In earlier times, slaves were treated no differently from goats or other animals. They were barely cared for, much less given gifts.

Who is Allah? He is مالک and سید. He is our absolute owner, with complete authority over us. That makes us His ملکیۃ: His property.

But Allah as رب is an owner unlike any other. He takes good care of His property. He has complete authority and control over it. He brings it to full growth. He looks after it, protects it, sustains it, and sets it on the right path. Then, beyond all of that, He gives it many generous gifts it did not deserve.

I have to take this seriously: Allah, as our master, owes us nothing. So whatever He gives us, in any form, is a gift. He is not obligated to give it at all.

I did nothing to deserve my eyes. I did not pay for my hand. No price can be placed on the eyes, even one eye. No price can be placed on the hands, or on the ability to walk. These gifts are beyond price.

Allah gave us all these precious gifts, yet He says He will question us about them on the Day of Judgment.

> ثُمَّ لَتُسْـَٔلُنَّ يَوْمَئِذٍ عَنِ ٱلنَّعِيمِ

On that Day, we will be asked about the greatest of all gifts: ایمان. The word نعیم is the superlative form of نعم, and it means the greatest gift of all.

What is that gift? It is the gift for which all the other gifts were given to us: ایمان. That is the ultimate gift: ایمان.

On the Day of Judgment, He will ask us about our ایمان, the greatest blessing of all. Without it, what would be the purpose of giving us these gifts and making us different from animals? We were given them so that we could see the creations, reflect on them, recognize Him, and try to draw closer to Him.

I now understand that Allah as رب is the One who:

- owns us;
- holds ultimate authority over us;
- trains us and nurtures us with love, kindness, and patience until we reach maturity;
- places us on His chosen path;
- then showers us with many undeserved gifts.

### QAYYIM

QAYYIM

![](images/78/f63ba209-ecbb-4b20-844c-b4731e0c1d62.jpg)

The final meaning of رب comes from قَیِّم: the one who ensures that things hold together and do not fall apart. It means the one whose attention and care remain fixed on what He sustains. Important things collapse when care is withdrawn. The matter is easy to see in ordinary life. People who garden know that some plants are extremely delicate; if they are neglected for even one day, they die. Some people keep fish as pets; if the fish are not fed for a single day, they end up belly-up. That night, there is NEMO KABAAB for dinner. A house left unattended for one day quickly shows what neglect does.

So قَیِّم means the Sustainer, the Maintainer, the One whose focus and attention are constantly upon things. That meaning is also embedded in رب. That is why Allah says in the Quran:

> اللَّہُ لَا اِلَٰہَ ھُوَ الْحَیُّ الْقَیُّومُ

Allah is the only الہ: الحی, the Giver of life, and القیوم, the One who then maintains that life through His constant attention to all things. The word قیوم is the superlative form of قَیِّم: the One who constantly sustains, constantly maintains, and never takes a break.

In other words, I am not alive because I keep myself alive. My next breath does not come because I eat well, exercise, follow a good diet, or live in a healthy place. None of that is the reason I am alive. I am alive because Allah allows me to live. He sustains me; my own efforts do not.

There are many ways my existence could end in a second: crossing a road, a small earthquake, a snowstorm.

The Indian actress Sri Devi died. She was a perfectly healthy woman, yet she drowned in a bathtub. She likely had plans for the next day, just as I do. She surely had short-term goals and long-term goals. All of them disappeared in a second. I exist because the قَیِّم allows me to exist. I am completely and constantly dependent on Allah.

I see another matter in this ayah: two attributes of Allah are mentioned, الحی and القیوم. He gives life, and He constantly maintains what He gives life to.

These two attributes belong together because the One who gives life also knows how to sustain it. He knows how long it should be sustained. He knows the stages and steps through which it must pass. He knows how and when it will end, and what will happen after it ends. The entire plan for every individual creation, not only for human beings but for all creation, has already been fully set by the قیوم.

That is part of the meaning of قَیِّم: to have a plan. Nothing can be begun or maintained without a plan. A person who starts a business does not choose the business by playing rock paper scissors. The whole plan must be considered first:

- the initial investment
- how the business will stay afloat
- how it will grow
- costs and expenses
- budget allocations

The whole matter is planned before the business even begins. It is impossible that Allah created us without a plan.

### RAB

I now come to the complete definition of the word Rabb.

> الرب ھو المالک والسَّیِّد و المُرَبِّی والمُرشِد وَالمُنعِم والقَیِّم

RAB

![](images/78/7a08c24b-14c5-4e03-baea-0851a90c77f9.jpg)

رب is the One who is مالک, the One with absolute ownership. At the same time, He is سید, with complete and unconditional authority.

At the same time, He is مربی. He causes growth, takes care of us, nurtures us, and protects us with love, care, concern, and mercy.

At the same time, He is مرشد. He brings us to physical and spiritual maturity, then sets us on His straight path and puts us to proper use.

At the same time, He is منعم. He showers us with gifts and blessings that are undeserved and beyond number.

At the same time, He is قیم. He sustains us for the full span of our duration.

Allah, as our رب, gathers all these meanings together, all at once. He is not one of these things at one stage of life and another at a different stage. He is all of them, at the same time. His ownership includes His authority. His authority includes His nurturing. His nurturing includes His guidance and direction. His guidance includes His blessings. His blessings include His sustenance, provision, and maintenance. All of this is present together. SubhanAllah.

I now tie this understanding of رب to a point already discussed: the man of pure essence, someone from the اُولُو الْاَلْبَابِ, who reflects deeply on the creation of the heavens and the earth, and on the alternation of night and day.

> إِنَّ فِى خَلْقِ ٱلسَّمَوَتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱخْتِلَفِ ٱلَّيْلِ وَٱلنَّهَارِ لَآيَتٍۢ لِّأُو۟لِى ٱلْأَلْبَبِ ‎۱۹۰‏ٱلَّذِينَ يَذْكُرُونَ ٱللَّهَ قِيَمًۭا وَقُعُودًۭا وَعَلَىٰ جُنُوبِهِمْ وَيَتَفَكَّرُونَ فِى خَلْقِ ٱلسَّمَوَتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ

I find him saying:

> رَبَّنَا مَا خَلَقْتَ هَذَا بَطِلًۭا سُبْحَنَكَ فَقِنَا عَذَابَ ٱلنَّارِ

Once he recognized that the heavens, the earth, and he himself must have a creator, the word he used for that creator was Rabb. His reflection on the creation of the heavens and the earth led him to that name. In what he saw, the creator was the absolute owner, with complete authority. He was nurturing and developing His creation with love, care, and mercy. He was setting everything toward its purpose. He was pouring out countless gifts and blessings upon every created thing. He was sustaining, maintaining, and providing for them continuously through time.

That is why he said:

> رَبَّنَا مَا خَلَقْتَ هَذَا بَطِلًۭا

Our رب, you did not create all of this without a purpose.

This was a beautiful and fitting conclusion, because Allah confirmed it in His revelation. The very first ayah revealed to Rasul Allah ﷺ was:

> ٱقْرَأْ بِٱسْمِ رَبِّكَ ٱلَّذِى خَلَقَ

I notice that the first revelation says: Recite with the name of your رب who created you. It does not say ٱقْرَأْ بِٱسْمِ اللہِ ٱلَّذِى خَلَقَ‎. In this verse, Allah introduces himself to his Messenger for the very first time.

When a person introduces himself, he normally gives his name, not one of his qualities. If I met someone for the first time, and I said: السَّلَامُ عَلَیْکُمْ I am a teacher; or السَّلَامُ عَلَیْکُمْ I can do 200 push-ups, the reaction would be: وَعَلَیْکُمُ السَّلَامُ, You're also a weirdo! That is not how introductions usually work. A person begins with his name.

For that reason, I would have expected Allah to say ٱقْرَأْ بِٱسْمِ اللہِ ٱلَّذِى خَلَقَ: Read with the name of Allah who created you. Instead, he says: Read in the name of your رب who created you. Allah chose to introduce himself as رب. That choice tells me something important.

When I introduce myself as a teacher, I am saying how I want to be known. I am presenting myself through that role. In effect, I am saying that being a teacher matters to me more than my other qualities, even more than my name.

Allah is glorious beyond comprehension. He is magnificent beyond comprehension. He is powerful beyond comprehension. Yet what He says to Rasul Allah ﷺ, and to us by extension, is that the most important relationship between Him and us is Rabb. That is how He wants me to think of Him: not as جلیل, not as عظیم, not as قوی, not as کبیر, not as متکبر, but as رب.

The weight of that choice needs to settle in the heart. When a king introduces himself, he does not usually choose a humble description. He does not say, “I'm your neighbor!” or “I'm your dad's friend!” or “I'm your caretaker.” He says, “I AM YOUR KING. KNEEL. BOW DOWN BEFORE ME!”

If Allah had chosen such a form of introduction in the Quran, it would have suited His majesty. It would have been entirely fitting. Yet the word He chose instead was رب. This shows how important the word is to Him. After the meanings of this word become clear, the loving relationship He wishes to have with His creation also becomes clearer.

This matters deeply for the theme of revelation. In the sequence of revelation, the very first verse says:

> ٱقْرَأْ بِٱسْمِ رَبِّكَ ٱلَّذِى خَلَقَ

But the very last revealed verse says to the messenger ﷺ.

> ٱلْيَوْمَ أَكْمَلْتُ لَكُمْ دِينَكُمْ وَأَتْمَمْتُ عَلَيْكُمْ نِعْمَتِى وَرَضِيتُ لَكُمُ ٱلْإِسْلَمَ دِينًۭا

On this day I have perfected your religion for you, and I have completed my blessings on you, and I have selected Islam as the way of life for you.

> فَمَنِ ٱضْطُرَّ فِى مَخْمَصَةٍ غَيْرَ مُتَجَانِفٍۢ لِّإِثْمٍ

I then come to the final part of the ayah:

> فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۭ

I have no doubt that **Allah** is All-Forgiving and All-Merciful. The revelation begins with رب and ends with the name Allah. This exactly matches the pattern of بِسْمِ اللَّہِ الرَّحْمَنِ الرَّحِیمِ. In that statement comes the name Allah, followed by His two attributes of mercy: الرَّحْمَنِ الرَّحِیمِ. The revelation then ends with غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۭ: the constantly forgiving and the constantly loving. It begins with His intense and constant love and mercy, and it ends with His constant forgiveness and constant mercy. SubhanAllah!

## Master Slave Relationship

### What is RAB?

I have examined the linguistic meaning of the word رب. I learned that a رب brings together six attributes:

> المالک والسَّیِّد و المُرَبِّی والمُرشَد وَالمُنعِم والقَیِّم

A رب is, first, مالک: the one who possesses absolute ownership. At the same time, He is سید, meaning that He has complete authority over us.

Within that complete ownership and unrestricted authority, He is also مربی: the one who causes growth. He cares for His creation, nurtures them, and protects them. He is مرشد, which means that He not only nurtures His creation until maturity, but also gives each created thing its purpose. He sets every creation on the path of its destiny and supports it along that path.

In doing so, He is also منعم: He showers His creation with countless gifts and blessings that they have not earned. Finally, He is قیم: the one who sustains and maintains them for the whole span of their existence.

All these meanings come together in the word رب. I also learned that the primary ingredient in Allah's ربوبیۃ is love. All six meanings rest on that one fundamental emotion of رحمۃ: intense, constant love and care.

> الرَّحْمَٰنُ عَلَّمَ الْقُرْآنَ

The Loving, the Intensely Merciful, taught the Quran. It is not اَلْجَبَّارُ, عَلَّمَ, or اَلْقُرْآنَ. Thus رحمۃ is the most essential element of Allah's ربوبیۃ. This is why, when a person of sound essence reflected on the creation of the heavens and the earth, the word he used to call upon his Creator was رب.

> رَبَّنَا مَا خَلَقْتَ هَذَا بَطِلًۭا

> رَبَّنَآ إِنَّكَ مَن تُدْخِلِ ٱلنَّارَ فَقَدْ أَخْزَيْتَهُۥ

> رَّبَّنَآ إِنَّنَا سَمِعْنَا مُنَادِيًۭا يُنَادِى لِلْإِيمَنِ أَنْ آمِنُوا۟ بِرَبِّكُمْ فَـَٔامَنَّا

He used the word رب because he saw the whole creation reflecting the many attributes of its Creator, ربوبیۃ.

I may meet a highly skilled electrician and watch him at work. From his knowledge and skill alone, I can conclude that he studied at a good electrical engineering school, or that a highly skilled teacher trained him in this work. I have not seen that teacher. I may not even know the name of the school he attended. Yet I can still reach that conclusion by observing his work. The reason is simple: in this respect, the electrician is the product of his teacher. Through his skill and knowledge, he displays the training, discipline, and qualities his teacher gave him.

All creation does the same. It continually displays the ربوبیۃ of its Creator. This principle is true at the smallest level, and it is certainly true at the largest level. The universe, because it is created, continually reflects the attributes of its Creator.

> تُسَبِّحُ لَهُ ٱلسَّمَوَتُ ٱلسَّبْعُ وَٱلْأَرْضُ وَمَن فِيهِنَّ ۚ وَإِن مِّن شَىْءٍ إِلَّا يُسَبِّحُ بِحَمْدِهِۦ وَلَكِن لَّا تَفْقَهُونَ تَسْبِيحَهُمْ

The seven heavens, the earth, and everything within them constantly glorify Him. There is not a single thing that does not sing His praise. But you do not understand their glorification.

In other words, the signs and آیات of who Allah is have been placed into the very fabric of His design. The more I reflect on creation, the more clearly I can appreciate the beauty of that design.

I said that رب is commonly translated into English as “Lord.” Linguists, however, agree that the closest single-word replacement for رب is “master.” A master does not only have ownership, authority, and control over something or someone. A master also possesses complete knowledge and skill. Those qualities are essential for nurturing, guidance, and maintenance. For that reason, **master** comes much closer to the meaning of رب than “lord.”

### Relationship

I now understand the meaning of رب, and the next point is that رب implies a relationship. Some words carry a relationship within them. They need a counterpart.

When I say “teacher,” the word already points to students. I cannot be a teacher if I have no students to teach. When I say “parent,” the word points to children. When I say “employer,” it points to employees. Certain titles require a relationship by their very meaning.

Other words do not work that way. When I say “intelligent,” the word does not necessarily place a person in a direct relationship with anyone else. It is a general attribute. The same is true of “tall.” It describes a quality, not a personal bond.

In the same way, Allah has certain names that do not imply a personal relationship. Allah is الأعلی. He is lofty. This is a powerful attribute, but it describes His state in relation to creation in a general way. It does not, by itself, establish a personal relationship.

Other names of Allah do establish such a relationship. خالق, the Creator, is one example. If He is Creator, then there must be مخلوق, creation. There cannot be a creator without creation. He can be powerful by Himself. He can be الأعلی on His own, but not خالق without creation.

غفور is another example. Allah is extremely forgiving, which means there must be someone to forgive. Some attributes of Allah are general in nature, while others imply a very personal relationship.

I therefore understand that when Allah begins His revelation with the word رب, and when He says اِقْرَأْ بِسْمِ رَبِّکَ الَّذِی خَلَقَ, He is introducing Himself as رب to Rasul Allah ﷺ, and through him, to us.

In doing so, what He is essentially saying is this: “When you think of Me, set all My other attributes aside for a moment, and think of Me first and foremost as your رب. Even if you forget every other relationship I have with you, never let go of this one. At every waking moment of your life, remain mindful that I am your رب. My ربوبیۃ will take precedence in all My dealings and interactions with you, and the primary ingredient of My ربوبیۃ is رحمۃ: love, care, and concern.”

I find that the statement does not simply say رب. It says ربک, “your” رب. Allah is the master of all creation. He is مالک and سید, the One who has absolute ownership and complete authority over everything. He could have said: اِقْرَأْ بِسْمِ رَبِّ الَّذِی خَلَقَ, “Read in the name of the master who created.”

But He does not leave it there. He makes it personal: اِقْرَأْ بِسْمِ رَبِّکَ الَّذِی خَلَقَ, “Read in the name of **your** master who created you.” It is as though He is saying, “I am **only** your master and nobody else's.” He makes His relationship that intimate with each one of us. SubhanAllah!

The relationship of رب requires a counterpart. If Allah is رب, if Allah is master, then I must ask what that makes me. What completes the relationship with a master is a slave. If Allah is master, that makes us all His slaves; and the Arabic word for slave is عبد. So when I accept Allah as my رب, by accepting that relationship itself, I am also accepting the fact that I am His slave.

I can accept this, once I think it through. I have already established that he is مالک: he owns us. I accept that Allah has complete authority over us because he is سید. I know that we live under his training and nurturing because he is مربی. I know that we need his guidance if we are to fulfill our destiny because he is مرشد. I know that we constantly receive countless gifts we never earned because he is منعم. I know, too, that he is our final sustainer and provider. We exist only because he allows us to exist, because he is قیم.

So it is not impossible, or even strange, to accept that we are slaves of this gracious master. The simple truth is that our acceptance does not change the fact. Whether we accept it or not, we are still his property and his slaves. My children could say to me, “We don't accept you as our father!” The answer would still be, “Well, tough luck. I am still your father.”

So I want to make the ground clear: we are slaves of this master whether we like it or not, and whether we want it or not. That is the relationship.

### Slave - ABD

Having said that, I want to show how beautiful this relationship with our master is. Until I appreciate the beauty of this relationship with him, I cannot gain the benefits of his ربوبیۃ. This matters because that is how relationships work.

I cannot receive benefit from a teacher until I acknowledge him and accept him as my teacher. I benefit only when I accept that relationship and submit to it.

I can see the same truth in the relationship between a doctor and a patient. A doctor cannot help a patient until the patient accepts the doctor's prescribed treatment and submits to it. If a patient has diabetes and the doctor tells him to stop eating candy, yet every time he sees candy, he says, “Oh, I know the doctor asked me not to eat it, but what does he know anyway? It is only a little bit. It is not as if I am eating the whole bag. He is never going to find out anyway. Besides, everyone else is eating candy, so why cannot I have it?” then the patient has not truly accepted the doctor-patient relationship.

It does not matter if he calls him Dr. So-and-so. It does not matter how many visits he makes or how many appointments he keeps. He will not benefit, because despite what he says, he has not truly accepted himself as a patient. Only when he fully accepts that relationship and submits to the doctor's treatment can he receive the benefit the doctor wants him to have.

The relationship works in both directions. What is striking is that the doctor, despite his vast knowledge and skill, is completely helpless here. The doctor has the knowledge to benefit the patient. The doctor truly cares about the patient. He wants to benefit him. Yet with all his knowledge, will, and desire to help, he cannot do anything, because the patient has not completed the relationship.

I am not really speaking about doctors and patients. I am speaking about myself. I claim that Allah is my رب. I know His infinite رحمۃ. I know that He wants what is best for me. I know the treatment He has prescribed for my soul. I know the duties and the prohibitions.

I may even pray and fulfill my religious obligations. Yet in daily life, I may still do the very things that go against His prescribed treatment. How, then, can my رب help me and benefit me? This is striking: despite the infinite powers of my رب, I leave the relationship incomplete, and so I fail to receive the benefit He wants for me.

If I truly want to gain the benefits my رب offers, I must ask what is required of me.

![](images/82/cfa7ce2f-61a4-4cb1-9479-ff4021731839.jpg)

I have to complete the relationship. I complete it by submitting myself as His عبد. His relationship with me is that of ربوبیۃ. I complete that relationship through my عبودیۃ and my عبادۃ: my submission, my servitude, and my worship.

### Worship - IBADAH

The words عبادۃ and عبودیۃ must be understood clearly. The word عبادۃ is generally translated as worship, and worship is an action. عبودیۃ is the inward state that makes me want to worship. عبادۃ are the different forms of worship. عبودیۃ is the feeling of servitude that drives those acts of worship.

IBADAH

![](images/82/11062766-b962-4709-ae7b-87ee1d9d0120.jpg)

What is striking is that, in Arabic, عبادۃ carries two meanings at once. It means عُبُودِیَّۃُ وَ الطَّاعَۃُ: worship and obedience together.

Worship is usually understood as prayer, fasting, ZAKAT, and similar acts. These are all forms of worship, but I often stop there and treat them as the whole meaning. That is the problem with translating عبادۃ simply as worship. Worship by itself does not necessarily include the idea of obedience.

I understand عبادۃ to include obedience, but not obedience produced by force. It is obedience that rises from love. The word obedience can carry a harsh meaning, because a person may be forced to obey. Children may be bullied into obedience through force or worse. In history, slaves were beaten and tortured into obedience like animals. That is why the word can carry a painful emotional weight.

But that is not the obedience meant here. Forced obedience is not عبادۃ. The Arabic word for that kind of compulsion is رِقَّۃ, which corresponds to غلامی in Urdu. رقۃ means forced obedience; عبودیۃ means willing obedience.

Worship is the outward form of that obedience. The seven forms of worship — وِلَایَۃٌ، طَھَارَۃٌ ، صَلَوَاۃٌ، زَکَوَاۃٌ، حَجٌّ، جِھَادٌ — are all forms in which obedience appears. I perform them because Allah has commanded them. Every act of worship, then, is carried out in obedience to his command.

When I obey willingly and happily, I am in a state of عبودیۃ, and that is the desired state. If I do the same act with resentment, without being truly pleased to do it, I am in a state of رقۃ, which is not the desired state.

The outward worship may be the same. I may perform صلواۃ in both cases. But if I pray unwillingly, it is no longer عبادۃ; it is رقۃ.

I arrive, then, at this: عبادۃ is nothing other than willing obedience to the Beloved, born from passionate and unconditional love. This is why, when اِمَامُ جَعْفَرٍ الصَّادِقِ was asked, یا أمام! ھَلِ الدِّینُ؟ - O Imam, what is the essence of religion? he said, اِلَّا الطَّاعَۃُ - nothing but obedience. Then the man asked: ھَلِ الطَّاعَۃُ؟ What is obedience? He replied الا الحُب - nothing but love. So the essence of every form of worship is willing obedience, and the essence of willing obedience is love.

![](images/82/281db322-3bb2-41ea-8b21-09f2524569b1.jpg)

I learned that Allah is ال الہ - He is “The الہ.” He is the One who takes precedence over every form of الہ in my life. But His relationship with me is more direct than that: He is my رب. The word Allah tells me who He is, and what He is meant to signify in my life in the broadest sense. The word رب describes His personal relationship with me. In other words, He is the One to be worshiped because He is اللہ, and He is also the One to be obeyed willingly because He is رب.

I can put this in practical terms. These two names matter because they shape ordinary life between the prayers. Suppose prayer is fifteen minutes away. Suppose فجر begins in fifteen minutes, and the عبادۃ still has to be made. What kind of عبادۃ is صلوۃ? It is worship. But from فجر until ظھر, a person may not be engaged in worship in the narrow sense. Even then, he is still meant to be obeying Him.

If I truly recognize Him as الہ, and if I truly accept His relationship with me as رب, then between two prayers I may not always be performing an act of worship, but I must still remain in obedience to Him. This is why the concept of عبادۃ is so comprehensive in Islam. Every form of obedience is counted as عبادۃ in Islam.

For that reason, when I explain the word عبادۃ, I prefer “obedience” over “worship.” Unconditional, willing obedience is worship.

A person earns a حلال income at work; that is an act of عبادۃ. A student goes to school and sincerely fulfills the purpose of learning; that is عبادۃ. A person obeys, loves, and respects his parents and teachers; that is عبادۃ. He is kind to his siblings, generous to the needy, and respectful to his elders; that is عبادۃ. He washes his hands before eating, covers his head before going to the toilet, smiles at someone, or makes وضو before sleeping; all of these are acts of عبادۃ when they are done in willing obedience and sincere love for the Beloved.

So the word عبادۃ in Islam is very comprehensive. It is not restricted to the formal acts of worship.

### Slave - ABD

I now turn from عبودیۃ to عبد, which means a slave. Many Muslims feel uneasy with the word slave because it brings a harsh image to mind. That image comes from the way masters have often treated slaves throughout history. I want to use those very examples as a contrast between the kinds of masters the world has known and the kind of master our رب is.

### Freedom of choice

I begin with the word slave. What is the defining mark of a slave? A slave is someone whose free will has been taken away. He has no choice in the decisions his master makes. He must obey the master's will without question. He has no independent existence except to serve the master's desires.

In worldly slavery, the master denies the slave's freedom and forbids him to choose for himself. That is the deepest humiliation. To take away the freedom of choice from an intelligent human being is to say, “I do not think you are capable of making the right choices.” It is to say, “You are no different from an animal to me. Just as I choose for my cattle and my dog, I will choose for you.” Denying a human being the freedom to choose is the height of humiliation and disgrace.

When I speak of our Master, I ask what the most precious gift is that He gave us. What is the final gift that sets us apart from every other creation in this world? It is free will.

Did He need to give it to us? No. He is مالک. He is my owner. He owns every created thing in this universe. That alone makes every creation His property and His slave. Being His property, or being His slave, therefore has nothing to do with being Muslim or not. Everything belongs to Him. Everything is His slave.

The sun is His property. The moon is His property. The galaxies are His property. This whole universe, and everything within it, including me, belongs to Him.

Does the sun have free will? Does the moon have free will? Do night and day have free will? No. They are all in complete submission to the will of their Creator.

> وَکُلٌّ فِی فَلَکٍ یَسْبَحُونَ.

They are all floating in their orbits. They have been bound by gravity. They have no choice except to float where they have been placed. Allah bound them and took away their freedom of choice.

Could He have done that to us? Could Allah have bound us? Could He have forced us into submission? The One who compelled and enslaved the sun, the moon, the solar systems, and the galaxies could certainly have forced us into submission.

But did He? No. He gave us intellect and freedom of choice, something He did not give to any other creation. SubhanAllah.

I see from the outset that Allah is truly my master. He is my رب, but His ربوبیۃ is unlike the mastery of any other master I know. Every worldly master first tries to take away freedom of choice. Teachers, parents, employers, courts, doctors: any worldly رب comes with some form of control, some restriction on the will.

Allah alone is the رب who has granted complete freedom of choice. If denying freedom of choice is the deepest humiliation, then granting full freedom of choice is the highest honor. SubhanAllah. Allah honored us above every other creation by giving us the freedom to choose.

> وَقُلِ ٱلْحَقُّ مِن رَّبِّكُمْ ۖ فَمَن شَآءَ فَلْيُؤْمِن وَمَن شَآءَ فَلْيَكْفُرْ

And say: "The truth has been revealed by your master." Then whosoever wills, let him believe, and whosoever wills, let him disbelieve

### Expectations

What gifts do masters give their slaves? Nothing of worth. They barely feed them, barely clothe them, and barely pay them. When they do provide food and clothing, they give only the minimum, just enough to keep them alive. Yet for the little they give, they expect a great deal in return. Every master I know gives the minimum and demands the maximum from his slaves.

I have spoken about what Allah has given us: the many undeserved gifts that the منعم has showered upon his creation. These are precious gifts, far beyond the ordinary logic of exchange. Any company that gave its employees so much would bleed red.

Measured against the quantity and quality of the gifts our رب has bestowed upon us, what does he ask in return? The bare minimum. The very least. SubhanAllah.

Among all created beings, our master has given us the most. The rest of creation remains constantly in عبودیۃ to him. They work around the clock, in constant obedience to Allah, without a moment's break, in order to serve us.

Yet although he has given us the most, he asks the least from us in return:

- one hour of صلواۃ each day;
- one month of fasting out of twelve, and even that only from sunrise to sunset;
- 2.5% of our wealth in زکواۃ, from wealth he gave us in the first place.

It is incredible.

And even then, I find mercy at every turn. If صلواۃ is missed, there is no problem; قضاء is there. If water cannot be found, there is no problem; تیمم is there. If illness or travel makes fasting difficult, the fasts are completed later. If a saved amount has not remained untouched for a full year, there is no problem; زکواۃ is not due. If there is no money for حج, or if the journey is unsafe, there is no problem; the obligation of حج is lifted from one's shoulders.

This is astonishing. How easy has he made it for us? What kind of master does this? I know of no other master in this world who makes matters so easy for a slave. I know of no other master who gives so much and asks for so little in return. Such a master is unheard of.

### Explanations

And if that generosity were not enough, he also sent revelation and messengers to explain his reasons to us. Does a master ever explain his reasons to his slaves? Can any slave demand, “Explain the purpose of this command to me”? Can a slave negotiate with his master? Can a slave say, “I will obey only if, and only when, the command is explained to me”? No. Yet this master explained matters to us in the Quran. Our master also showed us, through the living example of Rasul Allah ﷺ, how to carry out our obligations in practice. He did this to make the relationship easier to accept, and to make عبودیۃ easy for us.

> يُرِيدُ ٱللَّهُ بِكُمُ ٱلْيُسْرَ وَلَا يُرِيدُ بِكُمُ ٱلْعُسْرَ

Allah desires ease for you. He does not desire hardship for you.

### Who does it benefit?

I ask another question. Who are the acts of worship meant to benefit: Him or us? In every ordinary master-slave relationship, slavery is arranged for the master's benefit, not the slave's.

But this Master designed the universe for us. He gave us the most and asked the least in return. He even explained the reasons behind His commands. So the question becomes: everything He has asked us to do, whom does it benefit? It benefits us, and only us. SubhanAllah.

Every slave in the world is bought and put to work for the master's gain. Every command given to a slave serves the master's interest. What kind of Master is this, whose every command is for the benefit of His slaves? Not a single command is for His own benefit. If that is not love, if that is not care, if that is not mercy, if that is not ease, if that is not رحمۃ, then what is it? AllahuAkbar.

He is constantly serving us. And what He asks from us in return is also only for our benefit, not His.

### Judgment

I ask another question. Is a slave's work judged according to the slave's abilities, or according to the master's expectations? In ordinary slavery, a slave's work is judged by the master's standards and demands.

How does Allah judge our actions? He judges them according to each person's knowledge and ability. Nowhere in the Quran does Allah demand perfection. The only thing He asks for in the Quran is effort.

### Service

I know of no master in this world who rushes to meet the needs of his slaves. I have never seen even a story in which a slave cries out to his master in need, and the master drops everything and hurries to help him. Yet what does our RAB do?

> وَإِذَا سَأَلَكَ عِبَادِى عَنِّى فَإِنِّى قَرِيبٌ ۖ أُجِيبُ دَعْوَةَ ٱلدَّاعِ إِذَا دَعَانِ
>
> And when My slaves ask you concerning Me, Indeed I near him in that very moment. I immediately respond to the invocations of the supplicant when he calls upon Me.

No master ever does that. History shows the ordinary pattern: slaves are hurried forward to meet the needs of their masters, never the other way around. What kind of merciful master, then, is this, who hastens to fulfill the needs of His slaves?

I reflect more deeply on the ربوبیۃ of this master, and I see more clearly how astonishing his رحمۃ and mercy are. He is intensely loving, deeply caring, and overwhelmingly merciful. He is unlike any master I know, and unlike any master I will ever come to know in this world. No master does for his slave what our رب does for us.

Yet his subtle love and mercy can make me forget my place before him. I forget that I am his property. I forget that he is مالک. He is the owner. If he is the owner, then by definition I am owned property. He can do whatever he wills with me. He can break me, tear me apart, burn me, drown me. Nothing prevents him from doing any of it. And if he chooses to do so, nothing remains in my power, or in the power of all of us together, or in the power of everything in the universe combined, to protect me from his wrath.

> إِن يُرِدْنِ ٱلرَّحْمَنُ بِضُرٍّۢ . لَّا تُغْنِ عَنِّى شَفَعَتُهُمْ شَيْـًۭٔا وَلَا يُنقِذُونِ

If the Rehman decides to harm me, absolutely nothing in existence will be able to help me. That truth stands plainly.

> إِن يُرِدْنِ ٱلرَّحْمَنُ بِضُرٍّۢ

If the REHMAN decides to harm me. SubhanAllah. Not

> إِن يُرِدْنِ الجبار. إِن يُرِدْنِ القھار. إِن يُرِدْنِ ذوالنتقام

not the conqueror, not the powerful, not the avenger. If the Rehman decides to harm me: no, no, no.

> إِن يُرِدْنِ ٱلرَّحْمَنُ

If the REHMAN decides to harm me, then even in His ضُر, even in His harm, He is merciful. Hidden within it is a benefit. SubhanAllah.

And yet I constantly disobey Allah. I constantly fall short in my عبودیۃ, in my obedience and servitude to Him. Still, He allows me to exist.

My phone was causing problems. It was acting up and freezing on me. Every now and then I had to restart it. I went to the store and bought a new phone. I got rid of the old one. Why? Because it acted up on me occasionally, not all the time, only a few times. So I replaced it.

That is what is done with property when it no longer does its job. It is discarded. I belong to Allah. We all belong to Allah. And we are not doing our jobs, not merely occasionally, not merely sometimes, but in some cases constantly, all the time. What, then, should my Owner do with me? What should my Owner do with property as useless as myself? Discard it. But does He do that? AllahuAkbar.

So even when words such as رب and عبد (master and slave) are used, He is unlike any master imaginable in this world. This Master loves us intensely and constantly. When I realize that, my natural response is to love Him back. That is another unique relationship between a master and a slave.

### Love

I know no master in this world whom a slave naturally loves. History shows the opposite: slaves have hated their masters, and at the first chance they have wanted to run away from them.

But I wholeheartedly love my Master. The only direction I want to run is toward Him, not away from Him. I do that by submitting myself to His love and mercy, through willing obedience. This is not the forced obedience known in the world. It is obedience freely given.

This obedience rises from the highest stages of love already described. It appears as a desire for service and servitude, like the service of a passionate lover, an عاشق, for his معشوق - for his beloved. That is عبودیۃ. That is worship. In that lies my greatest challenge.

I face the greatest challenge, the greatest difficulty, and the hardest trial of every waking moment when I accept Allah as رب. This is the constant challenge I face, and every person in the world faces. It is not the acceptance of the Creator as Allah. That part does not trouble people. Even the جَاھِلِی عَرَبٍ accepted Him as Allah. The real problem, and the real test, is accepting Him as رب: as Master.

That acceptance carries its own demand. If I accept Him as رب, then I must become a slave. I must accept the relationship He has placed upon me. I must give up the false freedom of my passions and desires and wear the collar of His عبودیۃ, becoming His slave. That is where the struggle lies.

I live in an age of absolute and unrestricted self-indulgence. The spirit of the age says: I am in charge. I am in control. I am the master of my own destiny. I am powerful. I am wealthy. I do not have to listen to anyone. No one is the boss of me. I can do whatever I want, whenever I want. I am the king of my domain. This is my life. If I want to do it, I will do it. If I do not want to do it, I will not.

In other words, I make myself my own رب. I make myself my own master.

And I hear Allah saying: “No! I am your master. I gave you this life. I gave you this authority. I gave you the gift of parents. I gave you the gift of children. I honored you with intellect and free will. I gave you rank and power. I gave you health and strength. I gave you all these things so you would recognize Me and draw nearer to Me, because I am your master, and I am a master unlike any other master you will ever know. Submit to Me, and I will show you what true happiness is.”

My greatest challenge in this life is to accept my عبودیۃ willingly and gladly, and to submit to my master with sincere obedience and true worship. I can overcome that challenge only by recognizing and appreciating the love and mercy of my master. The relationship of ربوبیۃ becomes complete only when I submit willingly and obey this magnificent master, because love gives birth to love.

If this incredible رب does so much for disobedient and ungrateful slaves, what would He do for me if I truly became willingly obedient to Him?

> وَإِذْ تَأَذَّنَ رَبُّكُمْ لَىِٕن شَكَرْتُمْ لَأَزِيدَنَّكُمْ ۖ وَلَىِٕن كَفَرْتُمْ إِنَّ عَذَابِى لَشَدِيدٌۭ

And when your master said: “If you become grateful, I will give you more, but if you are ungrateful, then indeed my punishment is severe.”

### If Our Relationship With Allah Is Of Master And Slave, Then What Is The Concept Of Freedom In Islam?

What is freedom? حریۃ. In Arabic, freedom is very different from the way modern English usually defines it. In pre-Islamic Arab culture, the حر stood in contrast to the slave. A free person was someone who was not a عبد. Such a person possessed both حر and عبد.

One of the remarkable things about Islam is that it makes a person عبد اللہ. If I am عبد اللہ, the slave of Allah, then what is my relationship to everything else? I become free. My عبدیۃ, my obedience and servitude, belongs only to Allah. It does not belong to anything or anyone else. That is what sets me completely free. So the حر, the Arabs say:

> الحُرُّ عَبْدٌ مَا طَمَع

The free man remains a slave as long as he is covetous, as long as he is greedy, and as long as he still wants.

> والعَبْدُ حُرُّ مَا طَمَع

And the slave is a free man if he is content.

This reaches the essence of the Arab understanding of freedom before Islam. Real freedom was moral freedom. It was freedom from want, not freedom to do whatever I wished. In my culture, freedom has been defined almost entirely as freedom “to”: freedom to do whatever I want, freedom to say whatever I want, freedom to have whatever I want. For the Arabs, it was freedom “from”: freedom from desires, freedom from wants, and freedom from needs. That is how they understood freedom.

I understand Islamic freedom through this powerful pattern: I enslave myself to the One and become free from everything else, or I turn away from the One and become a slave to everything. Even enslavement to Allah is, in essence, the acceptance of His ربوبیۃ, because I am already a slave. I am His property whether I accept that truth or deny it. Freedom, then, begins when I recognize that relationship, draw nearer to Him, and through that nearness reach complete freedom and happiness.

### If Allah is so merciful then why did Imam Hussain and his friends suffer in Karbala?

The question of suffering depends on perspective. Did they truly suffer? The signs of suffering are fear, regret, depression, and excuses to escape.

The black slave John gives the example. He could have played the racist card, but he did not.

Karbala was the clearest example of عبودیۃ. It was a battle to defend the honor of the beloved. Yazeed had challenged the ربوبیۃ and الہیۃ of Imam Hussain's beloved, and Imam Hussain and his companions stood firm to defend that honor. They also set an example of true love for me. They showed that this is how love acts: it pays any price for the beloved. It sacrifices food, drink, children, family, and life itself, but it never deserts the beloved. Love that flees at the first sign of trouble is no love at all. For the true lover, what good is life without the beloved's grace?

> فَلَيـتَكَ تَحلُو وَالحَيـاةُ مَـرِيرَةٌ . وَلَيـتَكَ تَرضـى وَالأنـامُ غِضَـابُ

> وَلَيـتَ الَّذِي بَيـنِي وَ بَينَـكَ عَامِرٌ . وَبَـينِي وَبَيـنَ العــالَمِينَ خَـرَابُ

> فَيالَيـتَ شُـربِي مِن وِدَادِكَ صَافِياً . وَشُـربِي مِن مَـاء الفُراتِ سَـرَابُ

> إذا نِلتُ مِنكَ الوُدَّ فَالكُلُّ هَيِّـنٌ . وَكُـلَّ الَّذي فَوقَ الـتُّرابِ تُـرابُ

If I can only taste the sweetness of Your pleasure, it does not matter if life itself turns bitter for me.

If You are pleased with me, what do I care for the anger and wrath of the whole world?

May what lies between us remain beautiful, even if everything between me and the world turns ugly.

One sip from the wine of Your pure love makes the sweet water of the Euphrates seem like a mirage to me.

All I need is Your love. If I have that, I have everything. Without it, even the greatest treasure is dust to me.

## Understanding RAB Al-ALAMEEN

I begin with REHMA.

The classical definition of رب is:

> الرَبُّ ھُوَ المَالِکُ وَ السَّیِّد وَ المُرَبِّی وَ المُرْشَدُ وَ المُنعِمُ وَ القَیِّمُ

A رب joins six meanings in one. He has complete ownership and absolute authority. He nurtures, guides, and assigns each thing its purpose. He pours out undeserved gifts and blessings. He also sustains, maintains, and provides.

After learning what رب means, I must ask what he is رب of. In سُورَۃُ الْفَاتِحَۃِ, he tells us:

> ٱلْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ رَبِّ ٱلْعَلَمِينَ

I give all praise and gratitude to Allah, who is رَبِّ ٱلْعَلَمِينَ. I begin with the word ٱلْعَلَمِينَ, and then I turn to what رَبِّ ٱلْعَلَمِينَ means in practice.

### Linguistic analysis of ALAMEEN

Most English translations render الْعَالَمِینَ as “worlds.” They give the meaning as: all praise and gratitude belong to Allah, the master of the worlds, or the master of the universe. That English word gives only a weak and incomplete sense of the Arabic.

The word الْعَالَمِینَ is the plural of العالَم. To understand it, I first look at its counterpart, عالِم.

ALAM

![](images/103/eca60cad-bbc6-4834-8ec1-29abaedfcbd2.jpg)

The two words عالَم and عالِم have exactly the same spelling. The only difference between عالَم and عالِم is the فتحۃ and the کسرۃ. Apart from that, the spelling is identical.

In Arabic, when two words share a spelling, they also share a deep connection in meaning. Here, they also share the same root: علم. The word علم means knowledge. So عالِم is the one who knows, and عالَم is what becomes known to him.

The distinction is clear:

- عالِم is the one who knows; he is the cause.
- عالَم is what can be known; it is the effect.

The device through which عالِم comes to know عالَم is the intellect, which in Arabic is العقل.

I, as a human being, can know many things. Yet all the knowledge I gain over a lifetime, and all the knowledge human beings gather over centuries, still does not reach everything. I know some things, and I remain ignorant of others. Allah is unlike this. He knows everything that can be known. The word for the One who knows everything about all that exists to be known is علیم.

ALEEM

![](images/103/1b30e423-708e-4a37-82b8-2822340822cd.jpg)

It is the superlative form of عالِم. An عالِم is one who has knowledge, even if only some knowledge. But علیم is the One whose knowledge is absolute and complete, embracing everything.

So three words now stand from the same root, علم:

- عالَم: that which can be known; that which waits to be known, discovered, researched, and understood.
- عالِم: the one who studies, researches, discovers, and through that process learns about the عالَم.
- علیم: the One who knows everything there is to know.

Because I am limited in my ability to learn and understand, the علیم created the عالَم. It is there for study, reflection, and knowledge. Through it, awareness grows, and recognition becomes possible. Recognition of whom? The علیم.

COMBO

![](images/103/5a272c60-5c69-4412-acb0-543f36068b34.jpg)

Allah is infinite. Infinite means without limit. اِمَامُ جَعْفَرٍ الصَّادِقِ عَلَیْہِ السَّلَامُ explained Allah as:

> اَللّٰہُ اَکْبَرُ مِنْ اَنْ یُحَدَّ وَ یُحْسَرَ

I understand Allah's greatness in this sense: Allah has no boundary, and Allah cannot be confined within human comprehension. In other words, Allah cannot be “defined.”

Yet my human understanding works through definition. A definition gives finiteness to something. It limits a reality for me so that I can place it within a frame and understand it. This is what the intellect, or عقل, does. It sets a boundary. It confines. It defines.

This is the problem Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى solved for me: how can I understand and recognize an infinite Creator through finiteness? The answer is the creation of this عالَم. My ways of learning are limited. I must define something before I can understand it. So how can I, a creature of finiteness and definition, understand a Creator who is infinite and cannot be defined? I understand through the intermediary of a عالَم, which acts like an interpreter.

If a group speaks only English, and I know only Urdu, then I need a middleman to understand what is being said. I need someone who understands both languages, someone who can receive the message in English and translate it into Urdu for me.

The same idea applies to this عالم. In its creation, it is physical. In its design, it reflects the attributes of its Creator. It “defines” the attributes of the Creator's glory and magnificence, so that I can gain some understanding of my Creator.

### Universe Manifests existence of Allah

In other words, this universe becomes the medium through which I recognize Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى, for every sculpture proves the presence of a sculptor. The poet says:

> وُجُودُہُ لَہُ دَلِیلُ قَاطِعٌ

> حَاجَتُ کُلِّ مُحدَثٍ لِصَانعٌ

The strongest proof that Allah exists is that every sculpture requires a sculptor.

> وَلِکُلِّ عالَمُ لِحَاجَّتاً الی العَالِمٌ

In the same way, everything that can be known requires the existence of one who knows it, because nothing exists as a mere passive object. Therefore, I come to know that Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى exists through the آثار, through His signs and the effects He has left in creation. This is like the Greek philosopher who asked the Bedouin:

> بِمَ عَرَفتَ رَبُّك؟

“How did you come to know of your master?” he said.

> إن البِعرَةُ تَدُلُّ علی البَعِیر. والرَّوثَةُ تَدُلُّ علی الحمیر. و آثارُ القدمُ تَدُلُّ علی المَسِیر

He said: The camel's dung shows me that a camel passed here. The donkey's dung tells me that a donkey was here. Footsteps in the sand tell me that people walked through this place.

> فسمآءُ ذاتُ اَبرَاج. وَالْاَرْضَ ذَاتَ الْفِجَاجِ. واَبحَرُ ذَاتَ الامواج

So when I look at the sky, with its perfect constellations; when I see the earth, with its valleys and paths laid out with order; and when I look at the oceans, with all their waves.

> اَلَا تَدُلُّ عَلَی لَطِیفِ الْخَبِیرِ؟

Do all these not point to the presence of the لطیف and the خبیر? SubhanAllah. To him, it was that simple. I make such matters far more complicated than they are. The truth is that Allah's existence is the clearest and most evident truth for a sound and healthy فطرۃ. When that primordial essence of فطرۃ remains intact, the question of a creator's existence does not even arise. That is why, when the atheist says, “I wish there was a proof of the existence of Allah,” the believer replies, “I see nothing but the proofs of His existence.”

That is the linguistic meaning of the word عالَم. It is anything and everything that the عالِم can know, so that he may recognize the علیم. For that reason, “world” is not the best translation of عالمین. It narrows the word's range. If the word meant only “world,” then it would refer only to that, and to nothing beyond the world or the universe. Its plural would then have been العوالم, not الْعَالَمِینَ.

The word الْعَالَمِینَ covers a far wider range than the physical world alone. The statement رَبُّ الْعَالَمِینَ does not mean only that Allah is the master of worlds, or the master of the universe. It means that He is the master of everything that can be known. Everything in creation reflects the علیم. It exists in a passive state, waiting for the عالم to learn about it through reflection, so that he may recognize his Creator.

I therefore understand رَبُّ الْعَالَمِینَ in a much wider sense than “master of this world” or “master of this universe.” He is the master of every species, every distinct group within creation, every category, every concept, every idea, every postulate, every form of physical matter, every form of chemical matter, and every spiritual entity. Everything that exists, whether physical or metaphysical, falls within the meaning of عالَم. All of it exists for one reason alone: so that its Creator may be recognized and understood.

### Humility

I find many explanations of the term رَبُّ الْعَالَمِینَ. Here I draw out one interpretation, centered on a quality that is greatly lacking in society: humility. Allah points out that He created different cultures, different creations, different civilizations, and people of different languages. He says in the Quran:

> يَبَنِىٓ إِسْرَٓءِيلَ ٱذْكُرُوا۟ نِعْمَتِىَ ٱلَّتِىٓ أَنْعَمْتُ عَلَيْكُمْ وَأَنِّى فَضَّلْتُكُمْ عَلَى ٱلْعَلَمِينَ

I gave you preference over every other nation. The word used here for nations is عالمین.

I understand from this that Allah is the Creator and Master of all nations, and that His creation must therefore be treated with care. Race and religion do not remove that duty. No one may forget that Allah is رَبُّ الْعَالَمِینَ.

This matters because harm done to His creation is never small. A heart may be broken by words. A person may be tortured through a temporary authority that Allah Himself granted. In such moments, the warning remains: I am رَبُّ الْعَالَمِینَ.

Nor does this apply only to human beings. It includes animals, plants, and the earth itself. Before any cruelty is shown to any part of His creation, I must remember: He is their رب too.

When a slave is wronged, the offense ultimately reaches the master. In earlier times, wars could begin because someone from one tribe unjustly harmed or killed the slave of another. The master treated that act as a personal insult, because the slave was under his ownership and responsibility. Even if the slave had done wrong and deserved correction, the complaint still belonged before his master.

So Allah says, “I am رَبُّ الْعَالَمِینَ, so be careful in how you deal with My creation. It does not matter what group, religion, caste, belief system, or country they belong to. It does not matter what relationship they have with you on this earth.

“Before he is your son, he is My slave. Before she is your daughter, she is My slave. Before he is your father, and before she is your mother, they are My slaves. Before she is your daughter-in-law, she is My slave. Before he is your son-in-law, he is My slave. Before he is your employee, your servant, or your student, do not forget: he is My slave.

“I am present, and I am watching. If you treat them unjustly, if you hurt them, if you insult or degrade them, if you misuse your authority over them, you will answer to Me, because I am رَبُّ الْعَالَمِینَ.”

SubhanAllah.

In one of the sermons of نَہْجُ الْبَلَاغَۃِ, Maulana Ali explains what the title رب العلمین does: it brings all of us to the same level before the Creator. It marks all creation as His slaves.

No nation and no group is better than another because of race or color. If one person is better than another, then by whose standard is that superiority measured? Who alone can judge it? The Master.

This has become a serious disease in our community. Because we are Muslims, or because we are Sulemanis, we begin to think that we are better than the rest of the world.
